What is malloc doing, anyway?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

malloc has a very nice and simple interface: you ask for a certain amount of memory, and you’re given a void pointer to your block of contiguous bytes that’s been magicked into existence. Once you’re done with it, you call free with the same pointer and the memory disappears into the ether.

But what’s actually happening here? Obviously, as we’re running as a userspace program, malloc must be asking the kernel for the memory through syscalls. However, we may be allocating many small blocks, and syscalls are expensive (575 ns for an empty one, as measured below), so some bookkeeping is done in the background. We’ll demonstrate this on GNU libc (glibc) with the program snippet below, which calls malloc(16) a thousand times.

Using strace, we can see that the whole loop makes only two brk syscalls: the first asks where the program break is (the address where the process’s heap ends), and the second grows the heap by 132 KiB. These are both done in the first malloc call — the other 999 just take from that already allocated memory.

You may see both brk and sbrk mentioned in discussions. brk is a syscall that sets the program break to the address it is provided, and sbrk is a libc convenience wrapper that takes a delta, increasing the program break by that amount.

A running process gets its own address space, split into a number of regions. These include text for executable code, data for initialised global variables, and bss for uninitialised global variables, among others. The following program prints one address from each region of the process.

The addresses rise from the binary’s code up to its stack.

Some of these regions can change as the program runs. For example, malloc(16) grows the heap and moves the break up. Note that in this run, the break and printf in libc are 23.4 TiB apart, about 1,530 times the machine’s 15.6 GiB of RAM. Nearly all of that range is unmapped, so it costs no physical memory.

Because we can move the program break directly, we can write a little allocator of our own. This is called a bump allocator, because each call simply bumps up the amount of memory that we are allowed to use.

This allocator is not very good! It moves the break on every single request, and each move is a syscall. We can time a syscall against malloc to see why malloc requests large blocks from the kernel and cuts them up itself. Each of the following three functions is timed over a million calls.

sbrk(16) costs only 35 ns more than the empty syscall, whereas malloc(16) runs 22.7 times faster than sbrk(16).

The bump allocator also has no way to reuse freed memory. Each request lands straight after the one before, so in the following example, d goes after c even though b has been freed.

The rest of the post improves the allocator one stage at a time.

malloc actually allocates more memory than you request. This is because each allocation, or chunk, has a size header just before the returned pointer; this is how free works without passing an explicit size. Chunk sizes are always multiples of 16, so the low bits of the size are always zero, and glibc keeps three flags in them.

Each chunk is the request plus 8 bytes for the header, rounded up to a multiple of 16, with a minimum of 32. The rounding keeps every pointer that malloc returns 16-byte aligned, which is the alignment x86-64 gives max_align_t, so the memory suits any C type. The 32-byte minimum leaves room for the list pointers that a chunk stores once it is freed.

When we free a chunk, it is put into what’s called a free list to allow reuse. When more data is requested, malloc looks through this free list and splits large chunks to fit small requests, and free merges free neighbours back together. Each chunk in the free list needs two pieces of information: its size (which we already have), and a pointer to the next chunk in the free list. glibc stores the pointer after the size, reusing the memory that the user stored their data in before it was freed!

The toy allocator stores the same two fields in each chunk.

We can improve our my_malloc by rounding the request plus its header up to a multiple of 16 bytes, and taking the first chunk in the free list that is big enough. To keep things simple, the toy’s header is the whole chunk_t, so a chunk keeps room for next even while it is in use. That makes the header 16 bytes where glibc’s is 8, and my_malloc(100) takes a 128-byte chunk where malloc(100) takes 112.

We define take to split that chunk, leaving whatever the request does not need in the free list.

When no free chunk fits, grow_heap moves the program break up in 64 KiB steps using sbrk and puts the new space on the free list as one chunk.

With headers, we can now actually implement a proper my_free function! It steps back from the pointer to the header and hands the chunk c to insert_free. That function walks the list to find the free chunks either side of c by address, prev and next, and links c in between them. Then, if c ends where next starts, or starts where prev ends, the two become one chunk.

A test program allocates a, b and c, frees a and b, allocates d, then frees the rest.

Each line shows the chunk that the call allocated, if any, and then the free list. Every chunk is an [offset, size] pair counted from the start of the heap, and the offset is where its header starts, 16 bytes before the pointer that my_malloc returns.

d gets the chunk at offset 0, split from the 352-byte chunk that a and b merged into. The bump allocator put its fourth request after everything else. Freeing c merges three chunks into one, and freeing d leaves the single 64 KiB chunk that grow_heap added.

my_free reuses memory, but it never returns any to the kernel. Whether a free chunk still costs RAM depends on when the kernel puts physical memory behind an address. Each process sees virtual memory, which the kernel maps onto physical RAM in 4 KiB pages. That separation allows the kernel to hand out address ranges with no physical RAM behind them — a virtual page only needs to be backed by physical memory on its first write.

The following program moves the break up by 1 GiB, then writes one byte to every page and prints a row after each 256 MiB.

Virtual size counts the addresses the process has mapped, and resident set size (RSS) counts the pages that are in RAM. Straight after sbrk, virtual size is already just over 1 GiB, while RSS is 1308 KiB. Each 256 MiB written then costs 65,536 page faults, one per 4 KiB page, increasing the RSS.

Once a chunk’s pages have been written, they stay resident after my_free, until the allocator gives them back to the kernel.

We give memory back to the kernel by lowering the break. We introduce a new function, trim, that lowers the break once the free chunk at the top of the heap reaches 128 KiB, keeping 64 KiB so that the next few requests don’t move the break straight back up.

A test program allocates 1 MiB and then a 16-byte chunk above it, then writes to every page of the 1 MiB block. (Anonymous RSS counts resident memory with no backing file, which keeps libc’s code out of the numbers.)

Freeing the 1 MiB block leaves the break at 1088 KiB and anonymous RSS at 1124 KiB, because the 16-byte chunk above it is still live. Once that chunk goes too, trim lowers the break to 64 KiB, dropping the anonymous RSS to 160 KiB.

Only the top of the heap can be given back. A live chunk holds the break above it, so every free chunk below it stays resident, as the 1 MiB block did here. Over a long run, many allocations and deallocations leave free gaps between live chunks that later requests rarely fill, which is called fragmentation. These gaps stay resident, so in a long-running program, fragmentation can grow RSS until a memory limit kills the process.

The mmap syscall usually maps a file into the address space. With the MAP_ANONYMOUS flag there is no backing file, and the kernel creates a zero-filled, page-aligned mapping anywhere in the address space. Like the heap, its resident pages count towards the anonymous RSS. The munmap syscall returns a block with its own mapping to the kernel as soon as it is freed, whatever lies next to it.

To integrate this into our own allocator, my_malloc chooses between sbrk and mmap by the size of the request. Any request of 128 KiB or more, header included, gets a mapping of its own from map_chunk, rounded up to whole pages; smaller requests take the existing sbrk free list path.

For mmapped chunks, we use one of the three flags in the size header (M) to record that this chunk has its own mapping. my_free checks this bit to know how to free it. It unmaps a mapped chunk with munmap, and puts any other chunk back on the free list, where trim can release it.

Running the previous demo again, we see that freeing the 1 MiB block drops the anonymous RSS while the 16-byte chunk is still live.

The following diagram shows how this might look in the address space.

Why not mmap everything? Some allocators do! Even glibc creates extra heaps, called arenas, with mmap, and spreads threads across them so that they don’t all contend for the lock on the main heap. However, separate mappings have separate regions with no guarantee of contiguity, and chunks can’t be coalesced across these gaps. On the other hand, allocating with sbrk just bumps up the one contiguous region that you already have. A freed chunk that stays on the heap keeps its pages resident, so reusing it causes no page faults. Each new mapping starts with no physical memory behind it, and faults on every page it writes.

We compare the performance of our allocators against glibc’s malloc with a workload that repeatedly allocates and deallocates blocks of varying sizes1. To measure the speed, we find the average time of a step: free a random live block, allocate a replacement of random size, and write to each of its pages. At the end of the run, the live blocks hold 10.3 MiB. The last two columns give the anonymous RSS at that point, and again after every block is freed, which is the memory the allocator keeps.

my-bump-1 never frees or reuses memory, so its RSS is the 1425 MiB that the run allocates in total. Its 1.72 µs per step comes from an sbrk call for every request and page faults on new pages. my-freelist-2 reuses memory, so its RSS of 17.8 MiB is much closer to the 10.3 MiB of live data. However, each step walks the list twice: my_malloc walks it to find a fit, and insert_free walks it to find the chunk’s neighbours. my-trim-3 adds a third walk, because trim looks for the last free chunk on every my_free. my-freelist-2 and my-trim-3 run 65 and 142 times slower than glibc respectively. In return, my-trim-3 keeps only 0.188 MiB once every block is freed. my-mmap-4 calls mmap for large requests, which raises its time per step to 11.8 µs but gives it the lowest RSS with blocks live, at 11.2 MiB.

Why is glibc so fast? The slowdowns in our allocators are due to having to walk a long free list of chunks multiple times. glibc instead keeps free chunks in bins, separate lists by size, so a request can walk a list of appropriately sized chunks directly. It also keeps up to seven freed chunks of each small size in a thread-local cache (tcache) and hands them straight back to the next request of that size.

The tcache also explains the last column, where glibc keeps 19.0 MiB after every block is freed. The heap counts a cached chunk as in use, so one near the top stops glibc from lowering the break. The GLIBC_TUNABLES environment variable can turn the tcache off with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0, or fix the mmap threshold at 128 KiB with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.mmap_threshold=131072.

With the tcache off, freeing everything leaves 0.250 MiB, and each step takes 1.5 times as long as before.

glibc makes 51 mmap calls in the whole run, against the 5040 that my-mmap-4 makes. Freeing a mapped chunk raises glibc’s threshold to that chunk’s size, so later large requests come from the heap. With the threshold fixed at 128 KiB, every large request pays for its syscalls and page faults again, and glibc runs 10.7 times slower.

malloc spends memory to stay away from the kernel, parcelling out large blocks in userspace so that most calls never make a syscall.

The benchmark keeps 4000 blocks live, and each step frees a random block and allocates a replacement of a random size. Of the requests, 90% are 16 to 512 bytes, 9% are up to 16 KiB, and 1% are 128 KiB to 256 KiB. Each new block gets one write per page. An allocator runs 500,000 steps in a fresh process, built at -O2. Run on an Intel Xeon E3-1230 v3. ↩︎

Sony Indefinitely Delays Another PS5 Peripheral Days Before Release

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Sony has indefinitely delayed the PS5’s official Pulse Elevate desktop speakers, which were originally due to deploy days from now, on 12th November.

In a brief update on the PS Blog, the manufacturer said that it had “identified a production issue with the product and have made the decision to delay the launch”.

This delay feels eerily similar to the one that affected its FlexStrike arcade stick earlier in the year, which was originally due out on 6th August but ultimately indefinitely postponed.

At the time, the manufacturer said it had encountered “unexpected production delays”, but that was several months ago now and it hasn’t provided an update since.

Sony’s Edwin Foo said of the Pulse Elevate delay:

“The additional time will allow us to ensure the product will deliver the experience we intended for our players. We apologise for the delay and appreciate your patience and understanding. We look forward to bringing Pulse Elevate to players soon.”

The speakers – which are designed for both desktop and portable use – promise “lifelike, ultra-low latency” audio.

What’s particularly bizarre about this delay is that many outlets and influencers received review units earlier this week.

I think I’m allowed to say Push Square received a pair, and SpawnPoiint posted about them on Thursday:

I wonder what went wrong during production, and whether it’s going to have to scrap its entire inventory and start over from scratch on these.

Surely this close to its originally intended release, the $220 product must have made it into warehouses around the world already.

Democrats plan to push clean energy to lower bills and challenge Trump’s fossil fuel agenda

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Democrats can begin investigations and hold hearings into climate policy rollbacks and elimination of green energy grants by the Republican administration, potentially trying to undo some changes and restore renewable energy incentives.

Some Democrats are promising to push for more clean energy to help tackle rising energy costs when they take control of Congress in January, though President Donald Trump says he will use his veto power aggressively.

With new majorities in the House and Senate after last week’s midterms, Democrats can set an agenda boosting clean energy and addressing climate change. While those are traditionally core priorities of the party, climate was not a central topic in this election cycle.

Democrats can begin investigations and hold hearings into climate policy rollbacks and elimination of green energy grants by the Republican administration, potentially trying to undo some changes and restore renewable energy incentives.

Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island, the top Democrat on the Senate Committee on Environment and Public Works, said Trump’s clean energy attacks drove up prices for taxpayers. He said Democrats will “work every angle to lower consumers’ energy costs, and we will demand accountability for the massive corruption wrought by Trump’s fossil-fuel donors.”

American Clean Power Association CEO Jason Grumet said everybody who is trying to build clean energy “feels bolstered.”

Enacting major changes would require Republican support because Democrats will lack the numbers to override presidential vetoes. Even then, lower energy bills are not guaranteed because prices also depend on fuel costs, demand and the expense of maintaining the electric grid.

“The wins give Democrats more leverage over spending and more political power to advance a coherent agenda; the question is whether they’ll use that power effectively,” said Lisa Sachs, director of the Columbia Center on Sustainable Investment. “The vote was for affordability and opportunity. Democrats now need to be clear about which policies deliver them.”

Trump said after Tuesday’s election that Democrats will send over “things that are insane” and he will veto them, speaking generally about legislation. But in the comments to Fox News, he predicted that “after a couple of months of vetoes,” he and Democrats will find ways to work together.

The White House said in a statement Wednesday that Trump is “committed to unleashing reliable, affordable, and secure energy sources to lower prices, strengthen our grid, and protect our national security, and is willing to work with anyone to accomplish this.”

Trump promised to lower energy prices. Instead, his war with Iran and other policies increased the price of gasoline, diesel and electricity. At the same time, the administration canceled many clean energy projects and grants and slowed clean energy permitting.

Trump particularly dislikes offshore wind. Developer Orsted said Thursday that Revolution Wind, a large wind farm in federal waters off Rhode Island, has started operating commercially.

Will the president change his tune on green energy? That’s doubtful, said former Sen. Byron Dorgan, D-N.D., co-chair of the government relations practice at ArentFox Schiff.

“He is likely to keep pushing his energy issues, and I think he will be forced to give some room for a new Congress to recreate a different strategy that will add clean energy,” Dorgan said in an email.

Trump promotes expanding production of oil, natural gas and coal, which cause climate change, because he argues that would give the U.S. the lowest-cost electricity. Mike Sommers, CEO of the American Petroleum Institute, the oil and gas industry’s top lobbying group, said that as voters have changed the balance of political power in Washington, “the affordability message is unmistakable.”

Sommers said in a statement there is a real opportunity for bipartisan cooperation to reduce costs and strengthen the economy, starting with an energy permitting reform proposal announced in September. The legislation is aimed at shortening the timeline to get big energy projects started.

Ife Kilimanjaro, executive director of the U.S. Climate Action Network, said Democrats should reject that proposal and use their stronger position to pass legislation that actually builds out much-needed clean energy.

The administration has reshaped the Environmental Protection Agency, reversing pollution limits as it promotes fossil fuels.

Clear Street energy transition analyst Tim Moore said he expects Democrats to investigate the climate and environmental rollbacks, in an effort to block some from taking effect. Democrats could also try amending the Clean Air Act to cut carbon pollution and push back against EPA budget cuts, he added.

Rep. Sean Casten, D-Ill., said he is cautiously optimistic some Republicans will now seek to differentiate themselves from Trump and back pro-environmental policies. But without Republican support, Democrats’ more ambitious bills will largely lay out their agenda for 2028, he added.

Climate is not necessarily at the top of Democrats’ list of priorities. They are targeting healthcare access, trade tariffs and the Iran war.

Rep. Frank Pallone, the New Jersey Democrat who is expected to become chair of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, said the party should focus on healthcare, affordability, consumer and environmental protections, clean energy and regulating Big Tech.

David Victor, a professor of innovation and public policy at the University of California, San Diego, largely expects a stalemate at the federal level on climate and energy policy because of the divided government. He said agreement could come on some bipartisan measures such as a permitting overhaul.

Victor thinks Democratic-led investigations and hearings will center on corruption and other topics that may only touch on energy and climate. Like Casten, Victor said many of the Democratic bills likely will not become law, but will outline what Democrats could accomplish if they win the White House in 2028.

“The Democrats are going to be drafting things that the administration’s not going to like. They don’t have the votes to override a veto, and on top of it, the administration is going to be in turmoil because of its own electoral failures and because of all the investigations,” Victor said. “This seems to me, at the federal level, largely a recipe for kind of intense chaos over the next two years.”

One of the biggest wild cards is the behavior of the administration, which could use executive power to double down on rollbacks of climate and environmental rules, he said.

Democrats scored victories in races for governor in states long controlled by Republicans, including Texas and Georgia.

Leah Stokes, a political scientist at the UC Santa Barbara, said there could be a lot of state action on climate next year because of those wins. That is where climate leadership has traditionally come from, she noted. At the federal level, Stokes is optimistic about extending clean energy tax credits since some Republicans support that.

Jay Inslee, a former Democratic governor and congressman from Washington, also expects growing momentum for clean energy in states that elected Democratic governors, particularly in states where they also won legislative majorities.

“This is a really powerful instrument for the United States to join the rest of the world and get back in a leadership position, even if we still have a climate denier that might exercise a veto on some things that we want to do federally,” Inslee said. “So I hope people will pay attention to the big progress we’re going to make on a state-level basis.“By JENNIFER McDERMOTT Associated Press

Georgia is back at No. 1 in AP Top 25 ahead of Utah and Notre Dame; Ohio State drops to No. 21

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

UGA turned in one of the most dominant performances of the season.

Georgia was voted No. 1 in The Associated Press Top 25 for the second time this season Sunday, unbeaten UCLA matched its highest ranking in 25 years and Ohio State’s free fall continued after a wild weekend that followed the first College Football Playoff rankings of the year.

Miami became the fourth No. 1 team to get knocked out of the top spot, the most in a single season since 2008. Only one team â No. 6 Indiana, which was idle â remained in the same spot it had a week ago in the Top 25.

Georgia turned in one of the most dominant performances of the season in a 42-7 victory at Mississippi on Saturday. The Bulldogs had been No. 1 for one week when they were upset by Auburn on Oct. 17 but never dropped out of the top five, and they were the voters’ choice for No. 1 by a wide margin over Utah and Notre Dame.

Utah moved up one spot to No. 2 after its 28-20 win over rival BYU and the Utes now have their highest-ever AP ranking in the regular season. The Fighting Irish’s 31-24 win over Miami pushed them up one rung to No. 3, while the Hurricanes fell to No. 5 after their first loss.

Georgia earned 53 of the 70 first-place votes from the media voting panel. Utah received 12 first-place votes and Notre Dame got five.

UCLA is 9-0 for the first time since 1998 and moved up to No. 4. It’s the Bruins’ highest ranking since their 2001 team was No. 4 following a 6-0 start. After Miami, Indiana, Alabama, Texas, Tennessee and Texas Tech completed the top 10.

Alabama and Texas both were rewarded for road wins and moved up two spots. The Crimson Tide won at LSU and the Longhorns at Missouri.

Tennessee jumped from No. 16 to No. 9, its highest ranking in two years, after an impressive 34-6 win over a top-10 Kentucky. Texas Tech returned to the top 10 after outscoring West Virginia 53-48.

Ohio State, which was No. 2 two weeks ago, went from No. 11 after its loss at Southern California to No. 21 this week after a 41-28 home loss to Oregon. The Buckeyes hadn’t been ranked so low, excluding the 2020 pandemic season, since 2014.

Kyle Larson wins 2nd consecutive Cup Series title, becomes the 11th NASCAR driver with 3

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Larson was the overwhelming favorite to hoist the Bill France Cup in Homestead long before the green flag dropped.

HOMESTEAD, Fla. (AP) â Kyle Larson is now a three-time NASCAR champion.

Larson clinched his third Cup Series title in six years early in the 400-mile season finale at Homestead-Miami Speedway on Sunday. He became the first back-to-back champion since Jimmie Johnson in 2010; Johnson won five in a row beginning in 2006.

The 34-year-old Larson also became the 11th driver in NASCAR history with three Cup titles, joining a list that includes seven-time champs Richard Petty, Dale Earnhardt and Johnson. Jeff Gordon has four. Lee Petty, David Pearson, Cale Yarborough.

Darrell Waltrip, Tony Stewart and Joey Logano have three.

Larson was the overwhelming favorite to hoist the Bill France Cup in Homestead long before the green flag dropped.

He entered the weekend with a 67-point lead over Christopher Bell and needed only nine points to wrap up the title. He got one point for starting the race and seven more by finishing fourth in the opening stage. But because Bell didn’t win the stage, he had no path to catch Larson the rest of the day.

Larson responded to crew chief Cliff Daniels dropping the news over the radio by saying, “That was anticlimactic.”

Larson put himself in position for his latest title with a historic postseason, dubbed NASCAR’s Chase for the championship. He ended a 51-race winless skid by winning three of the previous nine playoff events and finished in the top 10 in eight of them.

He enjoyed a similar finish while winning it all in 2021, his first season at Hendrick. He won four of the last five races â including the finale at Phoenix Raceway â while still dealing with questions about how his time ended at Chip Ganassi Racing.

His second title came in stunning fashion last year at Phoenix. He took advantage of a late caution â his crew put on two new tires instead of four â and leaped sentimental favorite Denny Hamlin on pit road with two laps to go. Hamlin didn’t have enough time to catch Larson.

Larson’s third title was far less shocking. Securing the title was so much of a slam dunk that Larson was the only Cup driver invited to NASCAR’s championship media day Thursday, leading to a parade event that Larson called awkward.

The biggest question was how Larson and his No. 5 Hendrick Motorsports team would handle the pressure. Larson put that to rest during qualifying Saturday.

“They were debating on if a hot dog is a sandwich and if cereal is a soup,” Larson said. “So I would say they were pretty relaxed. I was surprised by all that.”

A better question: How does Larson compare to the best drivers in motorsports history?

Parnelli Jones, Mario Andretti, A.J. Foyt and Tony Stewart are widely regarded as the best of the best, guys who proved their worth by winning across varying racing series.

Jones won in sprint cars and stock cars around the same time he won the 1963 Indianapolis 500. Andretti won four IndyCar titles, the 1967 Daytona 500 and the 1969 Indianapolis 500. Foyt won four Indy 500s and the 1972 Daytona 500. Stewart won the 1997 IndyCar championship, three Cup Series titles and is currently racing dragsters at the top level, the NHRA.

Larson raced twice at the Indy 500 and has won the Rolex 24 at Daytona, three Chili Bowls and four Knoxville Nationals. And now he’s in elite company with three Cup titles.

“He wins in everything he does, and they’re all completely different cars and tracks,” retired driver and current NBC Sports race analyst Jeff Burton said. “And he’s racing against the best in every discipline.“By MARK LONG AP Sports Writer

Nvidia investigating reports that CEO Jensen Huang was targeted by Chinese intelligence

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The announcement came after Huang acknowledged to the board that he had been intimate with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence determined was gathering information for Beijing.

SAN FRANCISCO (AP) â Nvidia is opening an investigation into reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the “target of a foreign intelligence effort,” the company’s board of directors said Sunday.

The announcement came after Huang acknowledged to the board that he had been intimate with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence determined was gathering information for Beijing, The New York Times reported.

Huang apologized in a message to the company, according to the Times. “I was unfaithful to my wife,” he said. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Nvidia’s board said in a statement it has “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised,” but that its inquiry would examine all available information. Huang “fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it,” the board said.

Huang, 63, will remain CEO during the investigation.

The Times reported last month that U.S. intelligence agencies had discovered that Huang had developed a close relationship with Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen and chip buyer, and determined that she was feeding information directly to Beijing. The two are not related, according to the Times.

Alice Huang was previously a television reporter in China before becoming a top executive at Singapore-based data center company Megaspeed, which has become one of Nvidia’s biggest customers in Southeast Asia. Megaspeed bought more than $4.8 billion worth of Nvidia chips over the past two years, according to the Times.

Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

Crypto’s Next Two Years Should Be About Survival, Not Competition: Vitalik Buterin

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has warned crypto executives to switch focus to collective security and survival rather than competition between platforms. This follows multiple hacks that drained billions from the market and crushed traders’ sentiment.

In an interview with MoneyControl, Buterin urged crypto executives to focus on protecting user assets as a collective, as that could shape how regulators approach the sector. The next couple of years are key, as frameworks are still expected in several jurisdictions.

Digital asset firms, particularly centralized exchanges, have focused on gaining a larger share of the market cap, onboarding more users, etc but this buzz around gains leaves room for bad actors. For Buterin, the actions in the next couple of years will determine the long-term roadmap.

Most crypto users agreed with the Ethereum executive because, without asset safety, centralized exchanges will see capital exodus and more backing from traditional finance firms.

Recently, several crypto companies were hacked, including Binance, the largest centralized exchange by trading volume. Bad actors flagged in North Korea drained $6 billion; although large parts were recovered, it still remains the largest crypto hack of all time.

This week, Brazil’s largest exchange, Mercado, also suffered a breach that temporarily suspended trading. In a nutshell, these losses could prompt regulators to tighten requirements, setting the sector back.

Previously, regulators in the US and Europe often cited investor-protection concerns to delay major developments that would open the sector to traditional capital. Notably, spot crypto ETFs are a top example, although things changed after the White House backed the move following President Trump’s election win.

Buterin also added that recent attacks now target user interfaces and centralized components rather than smart contracts. He called for more operations to move on-chain, citing Binance Smart Chain and others.

“One good decision that Binance made is that they also launched Binance Smart Chain. They also benefit from the fact that if you do things with contracts on-chain, you can have very high levels of security. I am expecting we’re going to see more moves in that direction.”

On Ethereum security, Buterin and developers rolled out a roadmap to protect assets ahead of Q-Day. The network is lauded for taking early steps ahead of others as supercomputers get more powerful.

Echoes of Mirror's Edge, Dishonored and a dream set in Italy I once had can be found in Boars in Rome, an expressive parkour platformer set in dystopian Rome

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Years ago, I had this dream where I was parkouring through a greatly sunken version of Venice, no doubt sourced from the many hours I put into Assassin's Creed 2 as a teen who should have been studying for their GCSEs a lot more than they were. It's stuck with me after all this time mostly because of how much I wanted to capture that feeling again somehow, as the real Assassin's Creed didn't quite cut it. And lo and behold, there I was today browsing Bluesky when I came across Boars in Rome, a Mirror's Edge and Counter Strike Surf inspired parkour platformer set in a dystopian version of Rome.

Truthfully, witnessing the clip I've shared below was baffling to me. "This is what I dreamt of!" More or less, anyway, for the setting isn't quite the same; "You have been selected to start a new life off planet. Earth has been abandoned," explains the game's Steam page. "Machines keep working and nature claims what remains. Find out what happened while you were frozen in time by exploring the remnants of Rome where historical monuments mix with futurist megastructures."

Mirrors Edge x Counter Strike Surf where you parkour on anything! Be smooth n stylish in dystopian Rome. Or just jump around and explore, whatever works for you. It's called Boars in Rome! #PitchYaGame[image or embed]

Not all of it matches that dream of mine, some of it leans much more into brutalism than the typical, much older architecture you'd expect of Rome, but it's there nonetheless. In motion, Boars in Rome feels quite oneiric too. There is so much more control over the game's avatar than what my real body is capable of, or wants to do. You can run, jump, slide and wallrun, positioning it quite closely to VHOLUME mechanically. Where it differs is in its additives, movement tech like being able to swing on lampposts or vault over low down obstacles.

VHOLUME is more of a game about providing you with a simple toolkit and leaving you to push it to its maximum. Boars in Roam is a touch more expressive, and I'd dare to even call it an immersive sim. The levels you can romp around are linear, you have an end point to reach, only the journey is up to you. It certainly feels appropriate to put it in the im sim camp given that, at least the game's first level, calls to mind Viktor Antonov's work on Dishonored (and Half-Life 2, too, honestly).

As it currently stands, there's no release date for Boars in Rome, but there is a playtest you can sign up and gain immediate access to if you fancy a go yourself; you can do so on Steam right here, or at least pop it a wishlist.

Chromagraph Camera is exactly the camera app I’ve been looking for on iPhone

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Last week, movie director Rian Johnson announced a surprise new camera app for iPhone that reimagines what a smartphone photo should look like. Many people would agree that Apple’s iPhone photos are a tad bit overprocessed.

iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo introduced a new film texture option, but in my view, it’s a bit too exaggerated. Chromagraph Camera, on the other hand, feels pretty perfect.

Attempts have been made to improve iPhone photos for years, but they all feel a little bit too heavy, in my view. Most camera apps for iPhone require you to dial settings in. While that can be appropriate for certain settings, I ultimately want my iPhone to just be a point and shoot experience. If I wanted the perfect photo, I’d be using my camera.

That said, iPhone photos just don’t look super excellent out of the box. It’s not a limitation of hardware. If you take a RAW iPhone photo and edit it yourself, you can actually get surprisingly good results. Apple’s camera app just has poor defaults.

When Apple introduced the film grain feature on iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo, I was actually quite interested in it. It was certainly my favorite camera feature of the phone when I briefly tried out the iPhone 18 Pro. For some reason though, the film look just feels a bit exaggerated on iPhone Duo.

Apps like Adobe’s Project Indigo have gotten really close in terms of look, but the fact that there’s so much time spent post-processing means it’s often inconvenient. I’ve also had a few instances of losing photos because the app closed in the background while it was still processing the photo.

User reviews tell a different story. No trial option is a non- starter for me.

When Chromagraph Camera was announced, I was quite interested. I paid the $12.99, and I’ve been happy with the results. I tweeted some of my sample photos when I just got the app, and it’s been well worth it.

$12.99 may be a higher starting price, but if you really want an iPhone camera app that just takes good looking photos without a lot of hassle, it works really well.

Ian Cathro avait choisi de laisser Sohaib Naïr, en difficulté au Roudourou, sur le banc au profit de Kévin Pedro. Joao Ferreira reprenait le couloir droit. Pour le reste, le coach écossais revenait à son attaque Breum - Boakye - Ballo - Cardona.

Le Danois décalait Cardona qui ajustait le gardien lavallois (1-0, 45+1)

Les Verts avaient fait le plus dur au meilleur moment et allaient enfoncer le clou en début de deuxième période. En inversant les rôles : Cardona à la passe en centrant, Breum à la conclusion de la tête seul face au but à 3 mètres (2-0, 50e)

A partir de là, les Verts se contentèrent de gérer. Par deux fois, ils eurent l'occasion de tuer le match mais Davitashvili perdait son duel en frappant à côté du but et El Jamali se trouait de façon incompréhensible dans les 6 mètres alors qu'il n'avait plus qu'à conclure.

Pas de regrets toutefois pour les Verts qui profitent des revers de Pau, Montpellier et Reims pour reprendre un peu d'air au classement. Toujours en tête, ils devancent Nancy de 3 points avant de passer par la case Coupe de France.

Se arrivasse un risultato negativo non credo ci sia più la forza di tenere Allegri. Con la sosta vicina, se vinci e recuperi qualche giocatore, poi puoi rilanciarti. Ma se perde il Napoli, il presidente deve prendere una decisione".

Come ha visto lo screzio Lucca-De Bruyne?"Ma anche le parole di Politano sono state brutte. Non voglio dare colpe ad Allegri, ma se non tieni un gruppo unito qualche responsabilità ce l'ha anche lui. Per me comunque il presidente è intervenuto tardi".

Fiorentina-Juve sfida delicata."Partita molto interessante. La Fiorentina si è levata un grosso peso vincendo col Sassuolo, per muovere la classifica. Ci sarà un ambiente pazzesco, se la Juve non è messa bene, attenta, si rischia a Firenze. La Fiorentina davanti ha qualità, credo che la Juve sia più forte però incide il fatto che i bianconeri devono vincere per forza, invece la Fiorentina con la vittoria di Sassuolo può anche pareggiare. Dipenderà molto da come entrerà in campo la Juve, se sarà passiva rischia molto. Se la Juve vince, è un segnale importante. Non per lo Scudetto ma per la zona Champions".

Avoid these 5 common open enrollment pitfalls

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Whether you’re among the 53.5% of Americans who get their health insurance through work, the 20.1% enrolled in Medicare, or the 10.5% purchasing ACA coverage, mid-October or early November typically marks the start of the open enrollment period for next year’s insurance coverage.

Life would be easier if your health insurance coverage stayed the same if you liked it or adapted to your life automatically if you didn’t. Unfortunately, we mere mortals have to make decisions about our upcoming healthcare plan each year, in part because employers and other providers make regular changes to their insurance offerings—in addition to any legal changes that might affect federal benefits and ACA coverage.

The good news is that employers, Medicare providers, and the ACA marketplace typically time their open enrollment period to coincide, giving you November to make decisions about next year’s benefits.

But just because you know when to expect open enrollment season—who can miss all the open enrollment carols on the radio?—doesn’t mean you’re prepared for the important decisions you need to make. In fact, there are several common mistakes that could cost you big bucks in 2027 if you don’t know how to avoid them.

Here’s what you need to know about the most common open enrollment pitfalls.

The pitfall: missing a deadline

We all know how fast the fourth quarter tends to go. One minute you’re passing out candy to adorable Spidermen/Gwens and the next you’re eating Christmas cookies while making resolutions for 2027. The open enrollment deadline can get lost in the holiday shuffle.

For reference, these are the important deadlines for enrolling in a health insurance program during open enrollment:

  • ACA Marketplace has two enrollment deadlines:
    • December 15, 2026 for coverage beginning January 1, 2027
    • January 15, 2027 for coverage beginning February 1, 2027
  • Medicare: Enroll by December 7, 2026; coverage changes take effect January 1, 2027
  • Employer-sponsored health insurance coverage: Your deadline will vary depending on your employer. Your HR department will have the necessary information.

There are two possible problems you may face after missing an open enrollment deadline:

  1. You might have to go without insurance. If you miss the ACA open enrollment period, you can’t get insurance through the health insurance marketplace this year—unless you qualify for a special enrollment period. To qualify, you’d need to have gotten married or divorced, had a baby, moved, or lost your previous healthcare coverage.
  2. You might be reenrolled in the same plan. Medicare beneficiaries who miss the open enrollment deadline are typically reenrolled in the same plan if it’s still offered. But it may not be the best option for your needs.

Workers with employer-sponsored insurance plans may still be able to enroll after missing a deadline, depending on how draconian the HR department is—but it’s not a good idea to count on it.

The pitfall: procrastination

As busy as this time of year can be, it’s rare that we miss an open enrollment deadline because it slips our minds. Instead, it’s much more likely that we procrastinate until the last minute. Humans tend to put off decision-making that feels overwhelming, confusing, or deeply boring—which is precisely the kind of choices you need to make during open enrollment.

Luckily, we know how to beat procrastination. If you know you have open enrollment decisions to make but have been putting them off, follow these steps to keep from procrastinating yourself sick:

  1. Make it an appointment on your calendar. Set a specific time to deal with open enrollment paperwork. Schedule it in your calendar the same way you would a work meeting or doctor’s appointment.
  2. Gather necessary information beforehand. After you make your “open enrollment appointment” on your calendar, generate a list of the things you think you’ll need to make decisions. Start gathering this information a little at a time. Some of the data you might need includes the names, birthdates, and Social Security numbers of every member of your household, pay stubs or tax returns to show your household income, how much you paid out of pocket in 2026, and your preferred healthcare providers and medications.
  3. Spend no more than an hour on open enrollment. The reason we avoid tasks like open enrollment is that our brains convince us they’ll take forever. So give yourself permission to work for only 60 minutes. Stop at the end of the hour, even if you’re not completely done. Just schedule the next appointment before you quit for the day.
  4. Don’t let perfectionism thwart your decisions: Part of what makes open enrollment nerve-racking is the fact that it’s impossible to know ahead of time if you’ve made the best decisions for next year’s healthcare needs. Trying to find the perfect plan option that will maximize your healthcare coverage while minimizing your out-of-pocket costs can drive you nuts, since a year of robust health may follow a year of multiple ER visits. Instead of aiming for a perfect plan, base your decision on what’s probable for 2027.

The pitfall: making assumptions

We often try to reduce the complexity of tough decisions by making assumptions. Unfortunately, open enrollment is not a time when assumptions are your friends. It’s very common for healthcare plans to change their coverage and options from one year to the next, even when the plan itself keeps the same name or offered coverage level.

Here are some of the common changes that could catch you flat-footed if you’re not looking for them:

  • A revised formulary: Your plan may no longer cover the medication you’ve been taking for years—or it may start covering a medication that you’ve previously had to pay for out-of-pocket.
  • Increased annual deductible: Next year, you may have to pay more out of pocket before your coverage kicks in.
  • Revised coinsurance amount: Coinsurance is the percentage of the cost of a covered service that you’re responsible for paying, after meeting the annual deductible. Don’t assume that the coinsurance amount will stay the same from year to year.
  • Increased premium amounts for dependents: The monthly premium required to cover your spouse and children can often go up from one year to the next.
  • Increased out-of-pocket maximum: The maximum amount you might pay during 2027 may be higher than the limit set for 2026.

The pitfall: choosing based on price

Expecting a current plan will stay the same is not the only way assumptions could steer you wrong. It’s also easy to make assumptions about how well a plan will fit you based on cost.

For example, rather than compare options one by one, you might decide to enroll in the most expensive plan, assuming the higher cost means it’s better coverage. But the best health insurance plan for your needs depends on your specific situation. Springing for the high-premium/higher-coverage plan may be a waste of money for a marathon runner who sees the doctor only for an annual checkup. (Unless they tear an ACL, of course . . .)

But you might make a similar mistake in the other direction by signing up for the plan with the lowest premiums. It’s easy to assume lower monthly premiums will cost you less overall if you don’t consider the other costs you’ll have to pay. But lower premiums typically means lower coverage levels.

This price catch-22 can contribute to your procrastination, since it may feel like you’re going to overpay no matter what. (As a cynical veteran of open enrollment, I can honestly say that feeling is correct. You are probably going to pay more than you could have if you’d had a crystal ball.)

However, there are some questions you can ask yourself to help reduce the likelihood of making a decision-by-price mistake during open enrollment:

  1. Did my 2026 plan cover my healthcare needs? This gives you a baseline for determining if a new plan will suit your situation.
  2. How much did I spend out of pocket this year? If you spent more than you expected or more than you budgeted for, that can help you identify where you need more robust coverage.
  3. What confused me about my current coverage? Now’s an excellent time to clear up that confusion by contacting your HR representative, the ACA helpline at 1-800-318-2596, or Medicare at 1-800-MEDICARE (1-800-633-4227).

The pitfall: not checking your work

A jaw-dropping 90% of employers have open enrollment errors. These errors may include relatively minor problems like billing mistakes, as well as more major issues like eligible employees not getting enrolled despite opting in—leaving them without coverage.

Before you turn in your enrollment paperwork (or, more likely, hit the SUBMIT button), go back through the entire process to double-check that all the information is correct. Then, when you receive the documents, read through them for accuracy, as well. It’s much easier to fix a clerical error during open enrollment than after the new plan year begins.

Set yourself up for a healthy 2027

Open enrollment may rival filing taxes for the worst annual adulting requirement—but taking some time to make well-thought-out decisions for next year’s health insurance plans will always be something you’re glad you did.

5 tips for getting the most out of AI

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Why do some people seem to be getting a lot out of AI, while others—maybe you—still feel like they’re spinning their wheels trying to squeeze anything remotely useful out of these platforms? The answer is multifaceted, of course.

Some workflows are better suited to the strengths of large language models, while others are more vulnerable to their weaknesses. Some users pay for upgraded plans, which can improve how well the models work.

But a big part of the difference comes down to how you use artificial intelligence. Like any tool, there are ways to make it perform better for you. In other words, the technology is often what you make of it.

If you offload your thinking to AI platforms, you risk dulling your ability to approach problems creatively and becoming dependent on a flawed technological crutch. But if you use AI to challenge yourself, stress-test your work, and outsource routine tasks, you might feel, at least in some sense, like you’ve gotten a significant upgrade.

Consider the calculator: Some people have become so reliant on it that their mental math skills suffer, while others use the technology to tackle more advanced math because they’re not spending their time on simpler problems.

Like all new technologies, what you choose to outsource to AI is up to you. Still, if you do decide to use it, it’s worth considering how to get the most out of the technology—and make sure chatbots serve you, rather than the other way around. Here are a few tips to make these platforms work better for you.

Be smart about your prompts

What you type into your AI actually matters. Take traditional search engines. You may not realize it, but over time, you might have developed an implicit sense of how using certain Boolean operators or more specific proper nouns can yield better, more relevant results to your queries.

The same is true for AI. Being more specific with a chatbot about what information you want can make a big difference, particularly when you’re using the technology as a search tool. It might be easy to slip into thinking that AI should already know what you want, but it doesn’t. Instead, treat a chatbot as an earnest research assistant with the capacity to make a lot of mistakes. There’s an old adage: “The more you know, the more they know.” The same logic applies to AI.

Here’s an example: If I’m shopping for a bookshelf, I no longer sift through countless listings searching for something that feels right. Instead, I take a picture of the spot where I need it to go, give the AI the dimensions (or range of dimensions) I’m looking for, and ask the chatbot to find the best options given those constraints. This saves me a lot of time and allows me to work smarter when using AI for shopping.

Encourage humility

One of the big problems with AI—or any work you don’t do yourself and instead outsource to someone (or something) else—is that it will make mistakes. Fear of an AI oopsie is anxiety-inducing and absolutely real, and one of the best reasons not to completely offload all your tasks to ChatGPT or Claude. Really, don’t do this!

The best approach is to check any work produced by a chatbot yourself. But there are also ways to help these tools work better. If you have the ability to adjust your AI’s settings, tell it to be more humble—and more cautious—when sharing information or making claims. Tell your AI to consider multiple ways of thinking about a question or problem, and to present divergent viewpoints. For kicks, tell it to admit when it’s unsure.

Also, always ask for direct citations and check them off the platform. When you send a prompt to your chatbot, tell it to avoid simply summarizing information and instead to provide direct links to sources so you can verify the claims yourself. Once you do this, you might be shocked by how often AI claims to be consulting a source or generates a fake web page only to apologize when you discover it doesn’t actually exist.

A polite reminder: Just because Claude says it’s “double-checking” something doesn’t mean it actually is. You need to double-check it, too! AI can cite unreliable sources and even hallucinate citations entirely. AI is powerful, but also unpredictable, and should never be treated as an objective source of information. Think of it as a sieve that helps you sift through information and find what you want faster, rather than something you can trust to identify the best results on its own.

Understand memory

Your AI is remembering what you ask it and what you tell it, even if you’re not always aware. This isn’t necessarily unique to AI systems; many tech platforms collect all sorts of data about you and then use that information to tailor content to you (usually, ads). It’s possible you don’t want AI to remember anything about you at all, in which case, check your privacy settings—or perhaps avoid AI entirely.

But if you are comfortable with a chatbot retaining information about you, consider that its memory can actually be a useful tool. For instance, if you’re using AI to draft emails, tell your chatbot to always introduce you in the way you prefer. If you tend to offer to jump on a call or Zoom, tell the AI that you like doing so—and ask it to remember that preference for when you draft emails in the future. If you prefer a more casual tone, tell it that. If you prefer to remain formal, tell it that instead.

Here’s another example: If you’d like your AI to avoid sending you links from Amazon when you’re online shopping—perhaps you don’t have an Amazon account—tell it to remember that preference so you don’t have to keep sifting through those links. Or maybe you’d prefer your AI to prioritize links to The New York Times because you already have a subscription. Tell your AI that, too.

Also, remember that you don’t need to give AI your personal information at all. For kicks, try asking it to erase things from its memory that you don’t want it to retain. There’s absolutely no guarantee that a request alone will erase the information completely, so check your privacy and memory settings, too. But why not give it a stab?

Customize how AI communicates

You spend a lot of time on Google, but you might now be pivoting to AI to surface information instead. One advantage of this is that you can have AI produce results in all sorts of formats and styles, since you dictate the terms in which it expresses itself. For instance, you might prefer to receive information in simple bullet points rather than a long, essay-like string of text. You can share that preference with your chatbot.

Maybe you prefer visuals and graphics? AI is great at whipping those up. For the nerds: Maybe you prefer receiving information in code that you can then copy and paste into a development environment, like Python, so you can run a script to check whatever work the AI did in a more deterministic way.

There are even simpler changes you could make. You might prefer that AI respond in two languages, perhaps because you already know one but are working on developing your skills in the other. Seeing the text side by side as you interact with the platform throughout the day might help boost your fluency.

Another example: I’ve instructed my AI to regularly incorporate more unusual, niche vocabulary. There’s a somewhat well-founded fear that AI is homogenizing our language and making us less creative in how we express ourselves. I told AI to reverse that trend and gave it a list of common AI crutch words to avoid.

Know your way around tokens

AI operates on tokens. You spend input tokens on the information you give a model, including your prompts and relevant context, and output tokens on what it produces in response. You may not always know how many tokens you’ve used, but writing long prompts generally means using more input tokens, while receiving long responses means using more output tokens.

As you employ chatbots and run up against usage limits, consider that you can guide your AI on how much detail to provide for a particular task. Tell it to be brief when you want a short response or just one link, and tell it when you want a really detailed answer.

This simple, ingenious design keeps drones from breaking when they crash

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

At first glance, FlexiQuad looks like a small drone. Just one more buzzing thingy doing its flying thing. Then you see it crashing against a tree. A regular drone would crash and fall to the ground, but this one bounces back from the tree trunk and happily keeps flying along.

The key to this trick is a hollow ring design crafted from flexible materials.

Today, almost every quadcopter uses four stiff arms bolted to a central body that holds its battery, brains, sensors, and sometimes other payloads. ETH Zürich researchers trashed that core and the arms, placing the rotors on a ring, with the batteries and the rest of the electronics spread evenly around the rim. The FlexiQuad’s middle is empty, like a vertically flat donut.

The ring is flexible. That’s its superpower. Hit a wall and it buckles, absorbing the force of the impact and bouncing back. As the structure stabilizes, it keeps itself in the air. 

Developed by engineer Luca Girardi and Stefano Mintchev, a former professor at ETH Zürich’s Environmental Robotics Lab, the drone takes its design inspiration from how the wings of birds and insects bend in some directions but not others. “We were interested in how stiff their wings are and how they bend them—or don’t bend them—in different directions,” Girardi said in a statement. 

Wings yield in some directions but can also hold rigid in others. Engineers call this “anisotropic stiffness,” the resistance of a material or a structure to deformation, which changes depending on the pushing force’s direction. 

Girardi has worked on the idea for about five years. “At first, I was skeptical whether the drone would fly without the central reinforcement,” he said in the statement. “But I really liked the approach. Then we tried it—and it worked.”

“The key was to rethink where the weight sits, as well as where the structure bends,” Girardi tells me. “A heavy central body supported by flexible arms can introduce bending and oscillations.”

Turning the arms of a regular drone from hard to flexible would make the drone difficult to control. Soft arms will bend under the vertical force of the rotors. Even if they were hard enough to allow for takeoff, the wobbling would make it very difficult to control during flight. Once they moved the batteries next to the motors, Girardi explains, the central core of a traditional drone wasn’t needed. “The hollow center follows from that arrangement,” he says.

FlexiQuad’s donut can flex sideways, where the force of a collision will push. At the same time, it stays rigid vertically, which allows the propellers to lift the entire body without any wobbling or sagging.

Bouncing off the walls

The drone’s material flexibility was also key to the design. If it were too soft, the propellers would smack into each other during hard maneuvers as the ring deforms. Too stiff and you’re back to a breakable drone.

The researchers found the best flexibility range overlapped nicely with the stiffness of real wings, which works all the way from tiny insects to big birds. The approach works for drones weighing less than an ounce to up to about 6.6 pounds.

The other part of the design is the distributed mass. Birds and insects spread energy through their bodies instead of a central location. Hanging the weight of the batteries under the rotors gives overall stability to the design, according to the team’s preprint paper on arXiv, which hasn’t been peer reviewed yet. In simulations, an otherwise identical version with one central battery shook 13 times more at the rotors and 8 times more at the electronics during acceleration.

The result of the research is a 14-ounce prototype that is roughly a thousand times bendier than a conventional quadcopter. The drone tops 50 mph and accelerates at more than three times the force of gravity.

“Soft doesn’t automatically mean slow,” Girardi said in his statement. It can also tip into a sharp sideways dodge in the blink of an eye, which makes it as maneuverable as a regular drone. 

According to Girardi and Mintchev’s research, the design is four times more collision resilient than that of a regular drone. When it folds to fit through a gap, the propellers overlap and thrust efficiency drops from 79% to 68%, but it keeps flying.

In tests, FlexiQuad took head-on hits at about 11 mph without sustaining damage. The designers also found another benefit to the flexible body: It can fold itself thin enough to slip through an opening 70% as wide as its normal width. In one test, it went through a 7-inch slot. 

Its squeezing abilities can double as a grip, too. In another test, the drone hugged a soft first-aid pouch, carried it, and set it down. In another, it lifted a payload heavier than itself. 

The drone’s flexibility allows for easy transport: You can open the ring and lay it flat in a medium-size suitcase. Putting it back together is simple thanks to its slide-in batteries and quick-release fasteners.

The FlexiQuad’s inventors say it will be inexpensive to make. The frame is four thin strips of fiberglass laminate (the cheap material circuit boards are made of), for a total raw cost of less than $15, not counting motors, electronics, and batteries. 

Hoops in tight places

There are still (solvable) problems that need fixing. The drone has to learn to perceive its surroundings and navigate on its own. Also, after a hit, the bobbling of its body blurs camera images. The designers say they’ve have already used reinforcement learning to give the FlexiQuad an AI reflex that lets it steady itself after a crash with motion sensors, then resume flying without any pilot intervention. The frame’s bending itself might become a sense for the drone, too, they point out, the way insects feel their wings flex.  

Yet the design is so simple and effective already that ETH has nominated the drone for the university’s own Spark Award for invention of the year. Girardi and Mintchev have also applied for a patent, which envisages the FlexiQuad flying into places where a drone can’t avoid touching things, citing everything from a ship’s cramped ballast tanks to collapsed buildings in search-and-rescue missions and dense forests where scientists take measurements. 

FlexiQuad still has to turn into a commercial reality. Girardi tells me the team is commercializing it through a startup that, rather than selling only FlexiQuad, automates rapid drone design and ties it tightly to suppliers. The prototype sits at technology-readiness level 5 on NASA’s nine-step scale—meaning it’s been validated in realistic conditions—and is working toward level 6. 

The team faces the usual barriers to take it to market, Girardi says, such as how to turn the prototype into a commercial product, scale manufacturing with a positive margin, and ensure customers will buy it at the resulting price. But the market potential is so big, it’s hard not to see the FlexiQuad arriving soon. 

One instant application, which can be put to the test in the war in Ukraine to snatch kamikaze and rogue drones. Girardi tells me that they are about to start a field pilot program using a larger version with a net in its hollow center to catch unauthorized drones in civilian settings, “where you can’t afford kinetic interception—otherwise the debris risks [falling] onto people or sensitive infrastructure.” 

Another application, thanks to its gripping abilities, could be search-and-rescue work, which the team is exploring with firefighters.

And of course, I can see one of these under every Christmas tree. An unbreakable flexible hoop that flies. You know, for kids.

Hershey is making the 2026 holiday season sweeter with a fresh lineup of new and returning treats

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

If you already miss Halloween candy, never fear. The end-of-year holiday season is sure to be sweeter than ever, thanks to Hershey.

Fast Company was privileged to be part of a recent sneak preview of Hershey’s new and returning products for 2026, hosted by the confectionery giant’s “manager of merriment and magic,” Matt Spencer; director of marketing Alyssa Smith-D’Cruz; and associate brand manager Carly Andrews.

Here’s a look at the numbers and some of the new and returning products.

Which season is the most lucrative for Hershey?

While Halloween candy does get the most sales, Hershey’s profits are also boosted by Easter, Valentine’s Day, and the holiday season. Summer months even pitch in, thanks to everyone’s favorite campfire treat—the s’more.

Last year, Hershey grew its holiday share, generating $609 million in revenue. The company expects 2026’s figures to be broadly in line with prior years.

Shares of the Hershey Co. (NYSE: HSY) are down 2.37% year to date as of Friday’s market close, compared with a rise of 7.56% for the broader S&P 500. Hershey says it recognizes the current challenging economic situation that many consumers are facing.

“While consumers are increasingly thoughtful about how they spend, treats remain an accessible way to celebrate seasonal traditions and create moments of joy,” senior marketing director Samantha Siegal told Fast Company. “That’s why we continue to offer choice across pack sizes, formats, and price points.”

What’s returning from Hershey for the 2026 season?

Reese’s lovers rejoice. Both Reese’s Peanut Butter Trees and Mini Trees are back. Peanut butter and milk chocolate are the perfect combination.

Hershey’s Kisses are back with the Snickerdoodle Cookie flavor, Kit Kats are back with Peppermint Stick, and the Standard Milk Chocolate Bar is Grinch-themed. Don’t worry, though—the green guy’s heart is so big these days that he won’t steal your candy.

What’s new at Hershey for the 2026 holiday season?

Speaking of the Grinch, there are a couple of new characters making their debut in 2026. Cindy-Lou Who’s Hershey Kisses taste just like a strawberry sugar cookie. Max, the Grinch’s faithful canine companion, also wanted in on the fun with his new Hershey’s Hug edition.

Meanwhile, another furry friend is getting in on the action. Reese’s Reindeer Mix is made up of milk chocolate-drizzled popcorn, Reese’s baking chips, Reese’s Mini Trees, and Reese’s Pieces.

Also straight from the North Pole comes SkinnyPop Elf Maple Syrup Kettle Corn. Buddy the Elf approves of this salty and sweet treat.

You might not immediately think of these next two offerings as holiday treats, but it’s time to go outside the box.

Jolly Rancher Freeze-Dried Pouches contain cherry and green-apple flavors. Cadbury might seem like an Easter treat, but its Caramello Miniatures are the perfect end-of-year bite-size fun.

These are just some of the wonderful treats coming to a store near you. To Hershey, candy season is all year long. You can check out the full lineup here.

The ‘charismatic’ leader trap: Why organizations shouldn’t depend on one individual, no matter how appealing

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

When we think of a leader, charisma—an individual’s ability to motivate people to follow them, share their vision, and ultimately do what they say—is often at the center of our image. Charismatic, transformational leaders are respected, admired, and even loved. A successful business, however, is about much more than one person, and the danger with an overreliance on an individual is that too much power accumulates at the top of the tree.

No one person has the answer to everything. Without other viewpoints critical thinking diminishes, staff feel powerless to share their views, and danger arises of the culture becoming cultlike rather than collaborative.

Share the load

Collective leadership, where the load is shared and decisions are made through team-based processes, is both an ethical and pragmatic approach. It removes the risk that egos will run unchecked, and supports team motivation and autonomy.

Staff who feel they have a voice and impact on business direction are more invested and creative. This approach also serves to support the leader—as the saying goes, “It’s lonely at the top.”

Good collective leadership looks like this.

  • Sharing decision-making: from fostering a general sense that employees are involved, to creating formal structures for making decisions.
  • Distributing leadership roles: entrusting decision-making to experts, rather than necessarily those in the most senior positions.
  • Building leadership capabilities in others: facilitating opportunities for others to lead and making people feel empowered and safe to do so.
  • Creating the conditions for good thinking: coordinating an environment of collaboration.
  • Rewarding collective action: attributing achievement to everyone who contributed to a positive outcome, not just those in charge.

This is not to suggest that hierarchy is completely removed. Without any top-down structure, informal leaders will emerge, based on their own charisma, performance, and social standing. While responsibility can and should be spread, a good leader must remain accountable.

A step on from collective leadership is regenerative leadership, where responsibility is extended outside of a business toward the ecological and social systems within which all organizations are embedded; i.e., taking steps to improve local environments and the well-being of both staff and the wider community.

For the good of us all, this is the kind of leadership we should aspire to. Like-minded leaders seek each other out, and badges of ethics, such as becoming B Corp certified or employee owned, can help oil the wheels of success, all while creating a better world for us all to live in.

What we’re describing here is a “relational leader,” someone who understands that leadership is not a solo act, but the task of trusting, collaborating, and encouraging human connection.

Make and break sense

The relational leader’s job is not to have all the answers; it is to create the conditions where a team can make meaning together. People are more likely to commit to a new direction when they have participated in making sense of it.

Sense-making is always a social process, built through conversations and relationships. Sense-giving is the practice of helping others understand what is happening, why it matters, and how to move forward. Sense-breaking can be the most challenging, disrupting an existing understanding that is blocking progress. For example, a major pivot in a market where your business has operated for years.

What’s key is that conversations around sense aren’t subjected to gatekeeping, allowing team members to accept change and make faster, better decisions. Waiting for certainty before communicating can leave people to fill in the gap with their own stories, normally with narratives that are worse than reality.

Encourage difference

In business, different viewpoints and life experiences can enhance operations. While it takes significantly more effort, time and intentional leadership to make a diverse team work well, a wider range of skills, knowledge, and opinions will increase collective capability.

In the relational leadership model, leaders do not simply transmit a message downward; they enable dialogue among conflicting perspectives, navigating ambiguity and creating space for different voices to be heard. All of this supports collective sense-making. The emphasis here is not merely participation but preserving difference.

Teams that embrace difference are more likely to innovate and better understand the needs of different clients. What’s crucial is that people feel psychologically safe to share ideas, challenge authority, and admit mistakes.

Hold space for tension

Making space for difference by encouraging multiple voices is one thing; holding space for the tensions that arise when those voices pull in different directions is something quite different. In a traditional leadership model, equilibrium must be restored as quickly as possible. Tensions are treated with defensiveness and tried and tested solutions are favored that do not challenge the status quo—or the leaders’ authority.

Relational leadership draws on the “paradox mindset,” where competing demands sit alongside each and are given space to exist, rather than shutting down differing viewpoints. With this approach, issues can remain open for discussion without blocking progress; parked for review when time and resources allow.

Potentially, a pathway can be found that honors multiple truths, allowing for greater agility with room for a range of perspectives. It is often these moments of constructive friction that create new thinking.

Leadership for the modern world

In today’s world, where complexity and change are not passing conditions but the permanent backdrop of organizational life, leadership is no longer simply about authority or position. Building relationships, giving people space to excel and be themselves, and taking on a range of viewpoints are the keys to lasting success.
